codex
# CK-P0-001 diagnostic findings

No native root cause is proven. There are no Android tombstones, `adb logcat` traces, SQLite native errors, or deterministic device reproduction artifacts in the repository. The findings below separate verified code defects from ranked hypotheses.

A red-capable reproduction loop does not currently exist. Existing tests cannot detect a native crash, rollback failure, concurrent submission, or actual cross-loan database mutation.

## Execution trace

1. The Home action navigates to `/collections`.
2. The Collections screen loads every loan and borrower separately. It filters active loans, displays the borrower name through an in-memory lookup, and selects a loan solely by `item.id`.
3. Once selected, the screen previews penalties and earnings using that `selectedLoanId`.
4. Submission:

   - Parses whole INR using `parseInt(amountInr) * 100`.
   - Generates a new operation ID for every invocation.
   - Calls `recordCashPayment(selectedLoanId, amount, operationId, effectiveDate)`.
   - Does not disable the button while submission is running.

5. The repository:

   - Opens an Expo SQLite exclusive transaction.
   - Checks idempotency by operation ID.
   - Extends bullet schedules.
   - Loads schedules using `WHERE loan_id = ?`.
   - Assesses and reads penalties using the same loan ID.
   - Runs the pure allocation engine.
   - Inserts a collection journal row containing that loan ID.
   - Updates schedules using both `loan_id` and `installment_number`.
   - Settles only `loans.id = loanId`.

6. There is no success navigation in the implemented application. After completion, the screen sets an inline result, clears the amount, shows a native alert, and increments the global refresh counter. The selected loan remains selected.

Relevant paths: [collections.tsx](</F:/personal projects/credkeep/src/app/(tabs)/collections.tsx:15>), [transactionRepository.ts](</F:/personal projects/credkeep/src/core/database/transactionRepository.ts:9>), [allocation.ts](</F:/personal projects/credkeep/src/core/domain/allocation.ts:51>), and [DatabaseProvider.tsx](</F:/personal projects/credkeep/src/core/database/DatabaseProvider.tsx:29>).

## Verified defects and risks

### 1. The committed transaction implementation was not using the transaction handle

At the start of inspection, `recordCashPayment` opened:

```ts
db.withExclusiveTransactionAsync(async (tx) => { ... })
```

but performed its work through `this.db`, including schedule reads, journal inserts, penalty assessment, prepared updates, and settlement.

For Expo SDK 57, `withExclusiveTransactionAsync` creates a separate SQLite connection and passes it to the callback. The installed Expo source explicitly demonstrates using `txn.execAsync`, and `Transaction.createAsync` opens a new connection: [SQLiteDatabase.ts](</F:/personal projects/credkeep/node_modules/expo-sqlite/src/SQLiteDatabase.ts:168>) and [SQLiteDatabase.ts](</F:/personal projects/credkeep/node_modules/expo-sqlite/src/SQLiteDatabase.ts:783>).

Consequences:

- The journal insertion and schedule updates are not protected by the exclusive transaction that appears to contain them.
- An exception can leave a journal row committed without corresponding schedule changes, or vice versa.
- Concurrent payment attempts can read stale balances and calculate allocations from the same pre-payment state.
- Competing connections can produce lock/busy/deadlock behavior.

This is the strongest code-backed crash hypothesis, but it does not by itself prove a native process crash.

During this analysis, another process modified the working tree so `recordCashPayment` now uses `tx`. I did not make those edits. The uncommitted change is directionally correct, but it was not validated here. Similar misuse remains in `createLoanWithSchedule` and `runMigrations`, where `tx` is accepted but `this.db`/`db` is used.

### 2. Idempotent retries return an incompatible result shape

A new payment returns:

```ts
{ success: true, transactionId, result }
```

An existing operation returns the raw SQLite transaction row.

The UI unconditionally renders:

```ts
result.transactionId.substring(0, 8)
```

A successful duplicate/idempotent response therefore has no `transactionId` property and can throw a JavaScript `TypeError`. In a release build this may be perceived as an application crash, but it is not evidence of a native crash.

See [transactionRepository.ts](</F:/personal projects/credkeep/src/core/database/transactionRepository.ts:19>) and [collections.tsx](</F:/personal projects/credkeep/src/app/(tabs)/collections.tsx:164>).

### 3. UI-generated operation IDs do not provide retry idempotency

A fresh `crypto.randomUUID()` is created inside every `handlePayment` call. Rapid double-taps therefore produce two legitimate operation IDs, so the database uniqueness constraint does not stop a double collection.

The button has no `isSubmitting` state or disabled guard. Two overlapping transactions can consequently:

- Record two collections.
- Race while reading the same schedules.
- Encounter SQLite lock contention.
- Produce allocations calculated from stale balances.

### 4. Operation-ID reuse is not validated against the requested loan

The repository checks only whether an operation ID already exists. It does not verify that the existing row has the same:

- Loan ID
- Amount
- Effective date
- Payment method

If an operation ID is mistakenly reused for Loan B after being recorded for Loan A, the repository returns Loan A’s row as if Loan B’s request succeeded. This creates an apparent cross-loan receipt even though Loan B was not mutated.

### 5. The SQL mutation path is loan-scoped

Current payment SQL consistently includes `loan_id = ?`; the schedule update additionally includes `installment_number = ?`. I did not find a deterministic query that directly updates every loan or selects schedules without the target loan filter.

Therefore, a claim that the allocation engine intrinsically applies Loan A’s payment to Loan B is not supported by the inspected SQL.

The observed cross-loan behavior is more likely to involve one or more of:

1. Wrong loan selected in the UI.
2. Reused operation ID returning another loan’s transaction.
3. Concurrent, non-atomic execution causing misleading balances or receipts.
4. Stale screen state after refresh.
5. A defect present in device data or a code revision not captured by the current source.

### 6. Multiple loans are easy to misidentify

The selection card shows borrower name, principal, and only the first eight characters of the loan UUID. For one borrower with multiple loans of similar principal, there is no product, due amount/date, loan sequence, or outstanding balance to make the target unmistakable.

Because the repository receives only the selected loan ID, choosing the wrong card correctly posts to the wrong selected loan. That can be reported as “cross-loan allocation” even though the repository honored the supplied ID.

### 7. Success and refresh behavior is incomplete

There is a `Payment_Recorded.html` design artifact, but no corresponding Expo Router screen. The implemented screen only shows an alert and an inline result.

After `triggerRefresh()`:

- Loan and borrower lists reload.
- Penalty preview reloads.
- The selected loan remains selected.
- If the payment settled the loan, the user can remain on that now-settled loan and submit another payment.
- The inline receipt is not reset on refresh.
- A second payment against a settled instalment loan can be recorded as unallocated surplus because `recordCashPayment` does not reject inactive loans.

There are also two filesystem routes for the same logical loan-details URL:

- `src/app/loan/[id].tsx`
- `src/app/loan/[id]/index.tsx`

One observes `refreshVersion`; the other does not. This makes post-payment loan-detail refresh behavior ambiguous and should be resolved independently of the crash.

### 8. Input validation is insufficient

`parseInt` permits or mishandles several cases:

- `"12.50"` becomes ₹12 rather than ₹12.50.
- `"-100"` records a negative amount.
- `"0"` records a zero-value collection.
- Whitespace or malformed strings can become `NaN`.
- No safe-integer or maximum-value check is performed.

The schema has no positive-amount `CHECK` constraint, so invalid financial rows may reach SQLite.

### 9. Async preview failures are unhandled

Penalty preview and data-loading effects call asynchronous repository functions without local error handling. A database lock or query failure can become an unhandled rejection separate from the guarded payment submission.

## Git history findings

Relevant history from the local reflog:

- `d7722c6` — introduced the mobile application, Expo Router, and SQLCipher.
- `edcc4bf` — completed the V1 financial engine and penalty behavior.
- `05b5c43` — described as completing V1 end-to-end implementation.
- `a37bc13` — explicitly described as fixing a “transaction deadlock” and safely extending schedules.
- `6bb5068` — current recorded HEAD before the concurrent working-tree edits.

The deadlock-fix commit message confirms this transaction area had already produced a known locking problem. Nevertheless, the first version inspected still performed exclusive-transaction work through the parent database connection, indicating the correction was incomplete or subsequently regressed.

The ADR itself warns that transaction lifecycle mistakes can cause native bridge collisions: [ADR-0003](</F:/personal projects/credkeep/docs/adr/0003-relational-ledger-with-audit-history.md:34>).

## Ranked crash hypotheses

1. **Exclusive-transaction connection misuse / SQLite lock interaction — high confidence as a defect, unproven as native crash.**  
   Prediction: the failure rate should fall to zero when every operation in the callback uses `tx`, including nested repositories and penalty assessment. Logs should otherwise show SQLite busy/locked, transaction-state, or bridge errors near submission.

2. **Duplicate/idempotent response shape causes a release JavaScript fatal — medium confidence.**  
   Prediction: invoking the repository twice with the same operation ID and rendering the second result throws at `transactionId.substring`.

3. **Rapid double-tap creates overlapping transactions — medium confidence.**  
   Prediction: repeated fast taps increase reproduction frequency; disabling submission after the first tap removes it.

4. **Invalid amount reaches the SQLite/native binding layer — low-to-medium confidence.**  
   Prediction: malformed, negative, extremely large, or whitespace input reproduces the failure more reliably than a normal positive integer amount.

5. **Navigation-related native crash — low confidence.**  
   No success navigation currently occurs, so navigation is not a credible cause of a crash immediately after a normal payment unless the device build differs from this source.

A native stack trace is required to distinguish a SQLite/SQLCipher/native bridge failure from a JavaScript fatal or process termination.

## Acceptance criteria

### Loan isolation

- Given at least two active loans, recording a collection for Loan A creates journal rows only with `loan_id = A`.
- Every schedule row for Loan B remains exactly unchanged, including paid components, status, dates, and row count.
- Loan B’s status, penalties, earnings summary, and outstanding balance remain unchanged.
- The same guarantees hold when both loans belong to the same borrower and have identical principal and schedule values.
- Payment confirmation visibly identifies the borrower and complete target-loan context before submission.

### Atomicity and correctness

- Journal insertion, penalty assessment, schedule updates, and loan settlement all execute through the same transaction-scoped connection.
- A forced failure after journal insertion rolls back the journal, penalty rows, schedule changes, and loan status.
- A forced failure after one schedule update also rolls back every preceding mutation.
- The repository rejects unknown, inactive, or settled loans.
- Payment amount must be a positive safe integer in paise.
- All financial invariants reconcile after every successful payment.

### Idempotency and concurrency

- A stable operation ID is created once per user intent and retained through retries.
- Two submissions with the same operation ID return the same normalized receipt and create exactly one collection.
- Reusing an operation ID with a different loan, amount, or effective date returns an explicit idempotency-conflict error.
- The payment button remains disabled while submission is in progress.
- Concurrent collections on different loans remain isolated.
- Concurrent collections on the same loan serialize correctly without lost updates or double allocation.

### Success and refresh

- The repository returns one typed receipt shape for both new and idempotently replayed payments.
- Success UI is shown only after the transaction commits.
- The success receipt includes the exact loan ID, borrower, transaction ID, amount, and allocations.
- Failure or rollback never navigates to success and never displays a success alert.
- Returning from success refreshes loan, borrower, dashboard, report, and schedule data.
- A settled loan is removed from active collection choices and cannot receive another standard collection.
- There is a single canonical `/loan/[id]` route.

### Stability

- Repeating normal payments on a physical Android development build and a release-like build produces no process death, unhandled promise rejection, SQLite lock error, or fatal JavaScript exception.
- Any claimed native fix is verified using a redacted `adb logcat` trace from the original failing build and the fixed build.

## Recommended tests

### Real SQLite integration tests — highest priority

Use an Expo/Android test harness with real `expo-sqlite`; do not substitute a loose Jest object or desktop `sqlite3`.

1. Create Borrower 1 with Loans A and B, plus Borrower 2 with Loan C.
2. Snapshot every schedule, loan, journal, and penalty row.
3. Pay Loan B.
4. Assert:

   - Exactly one new collection for B.
   - Only B’s expected schedule rows changed.
   - A and C are deeply equal to their snapshots.
   - Journal allocation totals equal the schedule-component deltas.
   - Cash amount equals allocated principal + markup + penalty + surplus.

Repeat with A and B having identical schedules.

### Rollback fault-injection tests

Inject failures at these points:

- After penalty assessment.
- After collection journal insertion.
- After the first schedule update.
- Immediately before loan settlement.

After each failure, assert the complete database equals the pre-operation snapshot.

### Idempotency tests

- Same operation ID and identical request: one journal row, identical typed receipt.
- Same operation ID with a different loan: explicit conflict, no mutation.
- Same operation ID with a different amount/date: explicit conflict, no mutation.
- Simulated app restart followed by replay: still exactly one collection.

### Concurrency tests

- Two simultaneous calls with the same operation ID.
- Two simultaneous calls with different IDs against the same loan.
- Simultaneous calls against different loans.
- Rapid 20–100 submission stress loop, asserting invariants after every run.

### Allocation unit tests

Retain the existing pure allocation tests, adding:

- Input kists are not mutated.
- Results are deterministic for identical inputs.
- Partial payments preserve `principalPaid + markupPaid === amountPaid`.
- Future-kist allocation never crosses the provided loan-state collection.
- Negative, zero, `NaN`, fractional, and unsafe values are rejected before allocation.

### React Native component tests

Mock heavy native modules, but assert UI behavior:

- Selecting a card submits its exact loan ID.
- Two same-borrower loans remain distinguishable.
- Submit is disabled after the first press.
- A second press cannot generate another operation ID.
- Success renders a normalized new-payment response and an idempotent replay response.
- Failure leaves the user on the form and does not refresh or navigate.
- Refresh after settlement clears the selected loan.
- Loading and error states render without unhandled rejections.

Also add render-count assertions around `refreshVersion` so one payment does not cause unnecessary repeated database queries.

### Android crash reproduction

Create a small development-build scenario with two same-borrower loans and capture:

- Exact tap sequence and timestamps.
- Payment amount and selected loan IDs.
- Redacted `adb logcat`.
- JavaScript console output.
- Database snapshots before and after.
- Whether the process died, React Native showed a fatal JS error, or SQLite returned an ordinary rejected promise.

Loop single taps, double taps, same-operation retries, malformed inputs, and back-to-back payments until the failing category is identified.

## Current test gap

The existing allocation tests cover pure arithmetic only. The existing repository “integration” test uses mocks and does not exercise Expo SQLite transactions. A concurrently added untracked `cross-loan.test.ts` only inspects the loan-ID parameter in mocked SQL; it cannot detect transaction-connection misuse, rollback failure, native locking, or actual cross-loan mutation.

I could not execute Jest, TypeScript, or lint during this diagnostic run because Node-based command launches were rejected by the local execution helper before the processes started. No source changes or commits were made by this analysis.

# CK-P0-001 diagnostic findings

No native root cause is proven. There are no Android tombstones, `adb logcat` traces, SQLite native errors, or deterministic device reproduction artifacts in the repository. The findings below separate verified code defects from ranked hypotheses.

A red-capable reproduction loop does not currently exist. Existing tests cannot detect a native crash, rollback failure, concurrent submission, or actual cross-loan database mutation.

## Execution trace

1. The Home action navigates to `/collections`.
2. The Collections screen loads every loan and borrower separately. It filters active loans, displays the borrower name through an in-memory lookup, and selects a loan solely by `item.id`.
3. Once selected, the screen previews penalties and earnings using that `selectedLoanId`.
4. Submission:

   - Parses whole INR using `parseInt(amountInr) * 100`.
   - Generates a new operation ID for every invocation.
   - Calls `recordCashPayment(selectedLoanId, amount, operationId, effectiveDate)`.
   - Does not disable the button while submission is running.

5. The repository:

   - Opens an Expo SQLite exclusive transaction.
   - Checks idempotency by operation ID.
   - Extends bullet schedules.
   - Loads schedules using `WHERE loan_id = ?`.
   - Assesses and reads penalties using the same loan ID.
   - Runs the pure allocation engine.
   - Inserts a collection journal row containing that loan ID.
   - Updates schedules using both `loan_id` and `installment_number`.
   - Settles only `loans.id = loanId`.

6. There is no success navigation in the implemented application. After completion, the screen sets an inline result, clears the amount, shows a native alert, and increments the global refresh counter. The selected loan remains selected.

Relevant paths: [collections.tsx](</F:/personal projects/credkeep/src/app/(tabs)/collections.tsx:15>), [transactionRepository.ts](</F:/personal projects/credkeep/src/core/database/transactionRepository.ts:9>), [allocation.ts](</F:/personal projects/credkeep/src/core/domain/allocation.ts:51>), and [DatabaseProvider.tsx](</F:/personal projects/credkeep/src/core/database/DatabaseProvider.tsx:29>).

## Verified defects and risks

### 1. The committed transaction implementation was not using the transaction handle

At the start of inspection, `recordCashPayment` opened:

```ts
db.withExclusiveTransactionAsync(async (tx) => { ... })
```

but performed its work through `this.db`, including schedule reads, journal inserts, penalty assessment, prepared updates, and settlement.

For Expo SDK 57, `withExclusiveTransactionAsync` creates a separate SQLite connection and passes it to the callback. The installed Expo source explicitly demonstrates using `txn.execAsync`, and `Transaction.createAsync` opens a new connection: [SQLiteDatabase.ts](</F:/personal projects/credkeep/node_modules/expo-sqlite/src/SQLiteDatabase.ts:168>) and [SQLiteDatabase.ts](</F:/personal projects/credkeep/node_modules/expo-sqlite/src/SQLiteDatabase.ts:783>).

Consequences:

- The journal insertion and schedule updates are not protected by the exclusive transaction that appears to contain them.
- An exception can leave a journal row committed without corresponding schedule changes, or vice versa.
- Concurrent payment attempts can read stale balances and calculate allocations from the same pre-payment state.
- Competing connections can produce lock/busy/deadlock behavior.

This is the strongest code-backed crash hypothesis, but it does not by itself prove a native process crash.

During this analysis, another process modified the working tree so `recordCashPayment` now uses `tx`. I did not make those edits. The uncommitted change is directionally correct, but it was not validated here. Similar misuse remains in `createLoanWithSchedule` and `runMigrations`, where `tx` is accepted but `this.db`/`db` is used.

### 2. Idempotent retries return an incompatible result shape

A new payment returns:

```ts
{ success: true, transactionId, result }
```

An existing operation returns the raw SQLite transaction row.

The UI unconditionally renders:

```ts
result.transactionId.substring(0, 8)
```

A successful duplicate/idempotent response therefore has no `transactionId` property and can throw a JavaScript `TypeError`. In a release build this may be perceived as an application crash, but it is not evidence of a native crash.

See [transactionRepository.ts](</F:/personal projects/credkeep/src/core/database/transactionRepository.ts:19>) and [collections.tsx](</F:/personal projects/credkeep/src/app/(tabs)/collections.tsx:164>).

### 3. UI-generated operation IDs do not provide retry idempotency

A fresh `crypto.randomUUID()` is created inside every `handlePayment` call. Rapid double-taps therefore produce two legitimate operation IDs, so the database uniqueness constraint does not stop a double collection.

The button has no `isSubmitting` state or disabled guard. Two overlapping transactions can consequently:

- Record two collections.
- Race while reading the same schedules.
- Encounter SQLite lock contention.
- Produce allocations calculated from stale balances.

### 4. Operation-ID reuse is not validated against the requested loan

The repository checks only whether an operation ID already exists. It does not verify that the existing row has the same:

- Loan ID
- Amount
- Effective date
- Payment method

If an operation ID is mistakenly reused for Loan B after being recorded for Loan A, the repository returns Loan A’s row as if Loan B’s request succeeded. This creates an apparent cross-loan receipt even though Loan B was not mutated.

### 5. The SQL mutation path is loan-scoped

Current payment SQL consistently includes `loan_id = ?`; the schedule update additionally includes `installment_number = ?`. I did not find a deterministic query that directly updates every loan or selects schedules without the target loan filter.

Therefore, a claim that the allocation engine intrinsically applies Loan A’s payment to Loan B is not supported by the inspected SQL.

The observed cross-loan behavior is more likely to involve one or more of:

1. Wrong loan selected in the UI.
2. Reused operation ID returning another loan’s transaction.
3. Concurrent, non-atomic execution causing misleading balances or receipts.
4. Stale screen state after refresh.
5. A defect present in device data or a code revision not captured by the current source.

### 6. Multiple loans are easy to misidentify

The selection card shows borrower name, principal, and only the first eight characters of the loan UUID. For one borrower with multiple loans of similar principal, there is no product, due amount/date, loan sequence, or outstanding balance to make the target unmistakable.

Because the repository receives only the selected loan ID, choosing the wrong card correctly posts to the wrong selected loan. That can be reported as “cross-loan allocation” even though the repository honored the supplied ID.

### 7. Success and refresh behavior is incomplete

There is a `Payment_Recorded.html` design artifact, but no corresponding Expo Router screen. The implemented screen only shows an alert and an inline result.

After `triggerRefresh()`:

- Loan and borrower lists reload.
- Penalty preview reloads.
- The selected loan remains selected.
- If the payment settled the loan, the user can remain on that now-settled loan and submit another payment.
- The inline receipt is not reset on refresh.
- A second payment against a settled instalment loan can be recorded as unallocated surplus because `recordCashPayment` does not reject inactive loans.

There are also two filesystem routes for the same logical loan-details URL:

- `src/app/loan/[id].tsx`
- `src/app/loan/[id]/index.tsx`

One observes `refreshVersion`; the other does not. This makes post-payment loan-detail refresh behavior ambiguous and should be resolved independently of the crash.

### 8. Input validation is insufficient

`parseInt` permits or mishandles several cases:

- `"12.50"` becomes ₹12 rather than ₹12.50.
- `"-100"` records a negative amount.
- `"0"` records a zero-value collection.
- Whitespace or malformed strings can become `NaN`.
- No safe-integer or maximum-value check is performed.

The schema has no positive-amount `CHECK` constraint, so invalid financial rows may reach SQLite.

### 9. Async preview failures are unhandled

Penalty preview and data-loading effects call asynchronous repository functions without local error handling. A database lock or query failure can become an unhandled rejection separate from the guarded payment submission.
